Lie Theory — Lie Groups, Lie Algebras & the Classification of Semisimple Groups

Lie theory studies continuous symmetry — the mathematical framework underlying gauge theories, particle physics, and geometry. A Lie group G is a smooth manifold endowed with smooth group operations (multiplication and inversion). Its Lie algebra 𝔤 = TeG is the tangent space at the identity, equipped with a bilinear bracket [·,·] that satisfies antisymmetry and the Jacobi identity. The exponential map exp: 𝔤 → G connects infinitesimal generators to group elements. The classification of complex semisimple Lie algebras via Dynkin diagrams (An, Bn, Cn, Dn, E6, E7, E8, F4, G2) is a crowning achievement of 19th- and 20th-century mathematics. The Weyl character formula and highest weight theory describe all finite-dimensional irreducible representations. Applications permeate modern physics: the Standard Model gauge group SU(3)×SU(2)×U(1), the Poincaré group and Wigner's classification, and the exceptional group E8 in string theory.

"Lie groups are the mathematical language of continuous symmetry."
— Sophus Lie
Part 1

📏 Lie Groups and Their Lie Algebras

Foundations
Smooth Manifolds, Lie Brackets, and the Exponential Map
A Lie group G is a smooth manifold with smooth multiplication μ: G×G → G and inversion ι: G → G. The Lie algebra 𝔤 = TeG carries the Lie bracket [X,Y] = ad(X)(Y) induced by the adjoint action of G on itself. Explicitly, for X,Y ∈ 𝔤, [X,Y] = d/dt|_{t=0} Ad(exp(tX))(Y). The bracket is antisymmetric [X,Y] = −[Y,X] and satisfies the Jacobi identity: [X,[Y,Z]] + [Y,[Z,X]] + [Z,[X,Y]] = 0. The exponential map exp: 𝔤 → G is a local diffeomorphism near 0; for matrix groups, exp(X) = Σk=0^∞ X^k/k!. The adjoint representation Ad: G → GL(𝔤) is defined by Ad(g)(X) = gXg^{-1} (for matrix groups), and its derivative ad: 𝔤 → 𝔤𝔩(𝔤) satisfies ad(X)(Y) = [X,Y]. The Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula: exp(X)exp(Y) = exp(X + Y + ½[X,Y] + 1/12[X,[X,Y]] − 1/12[Y,[X,Y]] + ...). A fundamental theorem: there is a bijection between Lie subalgebras of 𝔤 and connected immersed Lie subgroups of G. Engel's theorem: a finite-dimensional Lie algebra is nilpotent iff ad(X) is nilpotent for every X ∈ 𝔤. Lie's theorem: every finite-dimensional irreducible representation of a solvable Lie algebra over an algebraically closed field of characteristic 0 has a common eigenvector — hence every such representation is 1-dimensional. The Levi decomposition states that any finite-dimensional Lie algebra is a semidirect product 𝔤 = 𝔯 ⋊ 𝔰 of its radical 𝔯 (maximal solvable ideal) and a semisimple subalgebra 𝔰 (the Levi factor).
The Lie algebra linearizes the group near the identity; the exponential map recovers local group structure, and the bracket encodes infinitesimal non-commutativity.
Part 2

🌍 Semisimple Lie Algebras and the Killing Form

Semisimple
Cartan's Criterion, Root Systems, and Dynkin Diagrams
The Killing form B(X,Y) = tr(ad(X)∘ad(Y)) is a symmetric bilinear form on 𝔤. Cartan's first criterion: 𝔤 is solvable iff B(X,Y) = 0 for all X ∈ 𝔤 and Y ∈ [𝔤,𝔤]. Cartan's second criterion: 𝔤 is semisimple (a direct sum of simple ideals) iff B is non-degenerate. A semisimple Lie algebra 𝔤 decomposes into simple ideals: 𝔤 = ⊕i 𝔤i, each with no nontrivial ideals. Choose a Cartan subalgebra 𝔥 ⊂ 𝔤 — a maximal abelian subalgebra consisting of ad-diagonalizable elements (a toral subalgebra). For 𝔤 = 𝔰𝔩(n,ℂ), 𝔥 consists of diagonal matrices with trace zero. The root space decomposition: 𝔤 = 𝔥 ⊕ ⊕α∈Δ 𝔤α where 𝔤α = {X∈𝔤: [h,X]=α(h)X for all h∈𝔥} and α∈𝔥^* are roots. The root system Δ ⊂ 𝔥^* is a finite set satisfying: (i) Δ spans 𝔥^*; (ii) α∈Δ ⇒ −α∈Δ; (iii) for α,β∈Δ, the reflection sα(β) = β − 2⟨β,α⟩/⟨α,α⟩·α lies in Δ; (iv) 2⟨β,α⟩/⟨α,α⟩ ∈ ℤ (integrality). The simple roots Π = {α1,...,αr} form a basis such that every root is a linear combination with integer coefficients all of the same sign. The Cartan matrix Aij = 2⟨αi,αj⟩/⟨αj,αj⟩ encodes the geometry: Aii = 2, Aij ≤ 0 for i≠j. The Dynkin diagram is a graph with r nodes (simple roots), with AijAji edges between node i and j (0,1,2,3) and an arrow pointing to the shorter root when edges > 1. The classification of complex simple Lie algebras yields four infinite families: An (𝔰𝔩(n+1,ℂ)), Bn (𝔰𝔬(2n+1,ℂ)), Cn (𝔰𝔭(2n,ℂ)), Dn (𝔰𝔬(2n,ℂ)); and five exceptional types: E6, E7, E8, F4, G2.
The Killing form detects semisimplicity; root systems provide a combinatorial blueprint for the entire structure of a semisimple Lie algebra, classified by Dynkin diagrams.
Part 3

🧮 The Weyl Character Formula and Highest Weight Theory

Representations
Highest Weight Modules, the Weyl Group, and Character Formulas
The Weyl group W is the finite reflection group generated by simple reflections si acting on 𝔥^*: si(α) = α − 2⟨α,αi⟩/⟨αi,αi⟩·αi. It permutes the roots and is isomorphic to the quotient NG(𝔥)/ZG(𝔥). The dominant Weyl chamber is the closed convex cone {λ∈𝔥^*: ⟨λ,αi⟩ ≥ 0 for all simple roots αi}. A highest weight module V(λ) is an irreducible representation with a vector vλ (the highest weight vector) such that: eα·vλ = 0 for all positive roots α, and h·vλ = λ(h)vλ for h∈𝔥. The theorem of the highest weight: every finite-dimensional irreducible representation of a complex semisimple Lie algebra has a unique highest weight λ ∈ 𝔥^* (dominant integral), and there is a bijection between dominant integral weights and isomorphism classes of irreducible representations. The representation V(λ) can be constructed as V(λ) = U(𝔤) / J(λ) where J(λ) is the left ideal generated by positive root spaces 𝔫+ and elements h−λ(h). The Weyl character formula: ch V(λ) = (Σw∈W ε(w) e^{w(λ+ρ)}) / (Πα>0 (1−e^{−α})), where ρ = ½Σα>0 α is the half-sum of positive roots and ε(w) = (−1)^{ℓ(w)} is the sign of w. The Weyl dimension formula: dim V(λ) = Πα>0 ⟨λ+ρ,α⟩ / ⟨ρ,α⟩. The Borel-Weil-Bott theorem: for a dominant integral weight λ, the irreducible representation V(λ) can be realized as H0(G/B, Lλ), the space of holomorphic sections of a line bundle Lλ on the flag variety G/B. More generally, Hq(G/B, Lλ) = 0 for q ≠ ℓ(w) when λ + ρ is regular, and equals V(w(λ+ρ)−ρ) for q = ℓ(w). The Kostant multiplicity formula: the multiplicity mλ(μ) of a weight μ in V(λ) is given by mλ(μ) = Σw∈W ε(w) P(w(λ+ρ)−μ−ρ), where P is the Kostant partition function counting ways to express a weight as a sum of positive roots.
Highest weight theory reduces the classification of irreducible representations to combinatorics; the Weyl character formula is one of the deepest results in representation theory.
Part 4

🔥 Real Forms and Applications to Physics

Physics
Cartan Decomposition, Iwasawa Decomposition, and Particle Physics
A real form of a complex semisimple Lie algebra 𝔤ℂ is a real Lie algebra 𝔤ℝ such that 𝔤ℂ = 𝔤ℝ ⊗ℝ ℂ. Every complex semisimple Lie algebra has a compact real form 𝔤u — the fixed points of a Cartan involution θ (a Lie algebra involution with negative-definite Killing form on the −1 eigenspace). The Cartan decomposition: 𝔤 = 𝔨 ⊕ 𝔭, where 𝔨 is the +1 eigenspace of θ (a maximal compact subalgebra) and 𝔭 is the −1 eigenspace. For SL(n,ℝ), the Cartan involution is θ(X) = −XT, giving 𝔨 = 𝔰𝔬(n) and 𝔭 = symmetric matrices. The Iwasawa decomposition at the group level: G = KAN (unique factorization), where K is a maximal compact subgroup, A is abelian (from a maximal split torus), and N is nilpotent. Real forms are classified by Satake diagrams (Dynkin diagrams with involutions and painted nodes): types AI, AII, AIII, BDI, CI, CII, DIII, EI–EIX, FI, FII, G, etc. Symmetric spaces G/K are Riemannian manifolds with a geodesic-reversing isometry at every point — these are the non-compact analogues of compact Lie groups. Applications in physics: the Standard Model gauge group SU(3)×SU(2)×U(1) is a product of compact Lie groups. Quarks transform in the fundamental representation of SU(3) (triplet) and SU(2) (doublet); leptons are SU(3) singlets and SU(2) doublets. The Poincaré group ISO(3,1) = ℝ4 ⋊ SO(3,1) (semidirect product of translations and Lorentz transformations) governs relativistic quantum mechanics. Wigner's classification: irreducible unitary representations of the Poincaré group are classified by mass m ≥ 0 and spin s ∈ ½ℤ (for m>0, the little group is SU(2); for m=0, the little group is E(2) — the Euclidean group in 2D). The spin groups Spin(n) are the double covers of SO(n); they are essential for describing fermions. The universal cover of the Lorentz group is SL(2,ℂ). The exceptional Lie group E8 plays a central role in the E8×E8 heterotic string theory — its root system has 240 roots and its Weyl group has order 696,729,600. The classification of conjugacy classes in the Weyl group and the study of nilpotent orbits (the Jacobson-Morozov theorem, the Bala-Carter classification) have deep connections to the geometry of the flag variety and the Springer correspondence.
Real forms of Lie algebras correspond to different signatures of invariant metrics; Lie theory provides the language for gauge theories, particle classification, and string theory.

🎯 Practice

Root Systems, Weyl Groups, and the Classification of Simple Lie Algebras

The classification of complex simple Lie algebras rests on the combinatorial data of a root system. Let 𝔤 be a complex semisimple Lie algebra with a fixed Cartan subalgebra 𝔥. The root system Φ ⊂ 𝔥* consists of non‑zero weights α for which the root space 𝔤_α is non‑trivial. Each root α defines a coroot α∨ = 2α/⟨α,α⟩ in 𝔥, and the integers ⟨β,α∨⟩ = 2⟨β,α⟩/⟨α,α⟩ are the Cartan integers. The simple roots Δ = {α₁,…,α_r} form a basis of the Euclidean space spanned by Φ such that every root is an integer linear combination of simple roots with coefficients all non‑negative or all non‑positive. The Cartan matrix A = (aij) with aij = ⟨α_j,α_i∨⟩ encodes the angles and length ratios between simple roots; it is a generalized Cartan matrix of finite type.

The Weyl group W is the finite reflection group generated by the simple reflections s_i(λ) = λ − ⟨λ,α_i∨⟩α_i. It acts faithfully on the weight lattice and permutes the roots. The Coxeter–Dynkin diagram of Φ has vertices for simple roots, edges labelled by the order of s_i s_j (3,4,6, or ∞), and arrows pointing from long to short roots when lengths differ. The connected diagrams of finite type are exactly the classical series A_n (n≥1), B_n (n≥2), C_n (n≥3), D_n (n≥4) and the exceptional diagrams E₆, E₇, E₈, F₄, G₂. Each diagram determines a unique (up to isomorphism) complex simple Lie algebra: A_n ≅ 𝔰𝔩n+1(ℂ), B_n ≅ 𝔰𝔬2n+1(ℂ), C_n ≅ 𝔰𝔭2n(ℂ), D_n ≅ 𝔰𝔬2n(ℂ), and the five exceptional algebras.

The classification theorem states that every complex simple Lie algebra is isomorphic to one of the above. The proof proceeds by showing that the Cartan matrix of a finite‑type root system must be positive definite, which forces the diagram to be one of the listed connected graphs. Conversely, for each diagram one constructs a Lie algebra via Serre’s generators and relations: generators e_i, f_i, h_i (i=1…r) with [h_i,h_j]=0, [h_i,e_j]=aije_j, [h_i,f_j]=−aijf_j, [e_i,f_j]=δijh_i, and the Serre relations (ad e_i)1−aij(e_j)=0, (ad f_i)1−aij(f_j)=0 for i≠j. The resulting algebra is finite‑dimensional, simple, and has the prescribed root system.

Important invariants derived from the root system include the dual Coxeter number h∨ = 1 + ∑α>0 ⟨ρ,α∨⟩/⟨θ,θ⟩ where θ is the highest root, the exponents m₁,…,m_r (the degrees of basic invariant polynomials on 𝔥 minus one), and the order of the Weyl group |W| = ∏i=1^r (m_i+1). For example, for E₈ the exponents are 1,7,11,13,17,19,23,29 giving |W| = 2·8·12·14·18·20·24·30 = 696 729 600. These numbers appear in the Weyl dimension formula, the Poincaré polynomial of the flag variety, and the eigenvalues of the Laplacian on the corresponding compact group.

The Peter–Weyl Theorem and Harmonic Analysis on Compact Lie Groups

Let G be a compact connected Lie group with normalized Haar measure dg. The Peter–Weyl theorem asserts that the matrix coefficients of all finite‑dimensional irreducible unitary representations of G form an orthonormal basis of L²(G). Concretely, if {π_λ} is a complete set of inequivalent irreducible representations with highest weights λ in the dominant Weyl chamber, and {vλ,i} an orthonormal basis of the representation space V_λ, then the functions √{dim V_λ} π_λ(g)ij = √{dim V_λ} ⟨vλ,i, π_λ(g)vλ,j⟩ constitute an orthonormal basis. The theorem has three equivalent formulations: (1) the algebraic Peter–Weyl decomposition L²(G) ≅ ⊕_λ End(V_λ) as a G×G‑module, where G acts by left and right translation; (2) the density of the *-subalgebra generated by matrix coefficients in C(G) with respect to the uniform norm; (3) the isomorphism of the representation ring R(G) with the ring of characters, which are class functions on G.

The proof uses the existence of a bi‑invariant Haar measure, the complete reducibility of unitary representations of compact groups (Weyl’s unitarian trick), and the fact that the convolution operator defined by a central function is a compact self‑adjoint operator on L²(G). Spectral theory then yields the orthogonal decomposition. The characters χ_λ(g) = tr π_λ(g) are central functions; they form an orthonormal basis of the subspace L²(G)^G of conjugation‑invariant square‑integrable functions. The Weyl character formula gives an explicit expression for χ_λ in terms of the Weyl denominator Δ(g) = ∏α>0(eα/2−e−α/2) evaluated on a maximal torus T ⊂ G: χ_λ|_T = (∑w∈W ε(w) ew(λ+ρ)) / Δ. This formula is the compact‑group analogue of the Weyl character formula for semisimple Lie algebras.

Harmonic analysis on G reduces to the study of the Fourier transform ̂f(λ) = ∫_G f(g) π_λ(g)^* dg, which maps L²(G) onto the Hilbert‑space direct sum ⊕_λ HS(V_λ) of Hilbert–Schmidt operators. The Plancherel formula reads ∫_G |f(g)|² dg = ∑_λ dim V_λ ‖̂f(λ)‖HS². Convolution corresponds to operator multiplication: (f∗g)̂(λ) = ̂f(λ) ̂g(λ). For the torus Tⁿ this recovers classical Fourier series; for SU(2) the irreducible representations are indexed by spin j ∈ ½ℕ, dim V_j = 2j+1, and the matrix coefficients are the Wigner D‑functions, which are spherical harmonics on the 3‑sphere S³ ≅ SU(2). The Peter–Weyl theorem underlies the theory of spherical functions on symmetric spaces G/K, where K is a maximal compact subgroup, and provides the foundation for the spectral analysis of invariant differential operators such as the Laplacian on G.

Worked Example: Decomposing L²(SU(2))
Show that the matrix coefficients of the spin‑j representation of SU(2) form an orthogonal basis of L²(SU(2)) and compute the norm of the character χ_j.
The irreducible representations of SU(2) are indexed by j ∈ ½ℕ with dimension 2j+1. Choose an orthonormal basis {|j,m⟩ : m = −j,…,j} of V_j. The matrix coefficient fm,m'(j)(g) = ⟨j,m|π_j(g)|j,m'⟩ satisfies ∫SU(2) fm,m'(j)(g) overline{fn,n'(k)(g)} dg = (1/(2j+1)) δjk δmn δm'n' by the Peter–Weyl orthogonality relations. Hence the rescaled functions √{2j+1} fm,m'(j) are orthonormal. The character χ_j(g) = tr π_j(g) = ∑m=−j^j fm,m(j)(g). Its L²‑norm squared is ∫ |χ_j|² = ∑m,m' ∫ fm,m(j) overline{fm',m'(j)} = ∑_m 1/(2j+1) = 1. Thus ‖χ_j‖L² = 1, confirming that characters are orthonormal in the subspace of class functions.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the decomposition of L²(G) for a compact group G, immediately write the Peter–Weyl isomorphism L²(G) ≅ ⊕_λ End(V_λ) and recall that the sum runs over dominant integral highest weights. For concrete groups (SU(2), SO(3), U(n)) know the parametrisation of λ (spin, highest weight) and the dimension formula. The orthogonality of matrix coefficients and the Plancherel measure dim V_λ are the two facts most often tested.

Cohomology of Lie Algebras: Chevalley–Eilenberg Complex and Applications

For a finite‑dimensional Lie algebra 𝔤 over a field of characteristic zero, the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex computes the Lie algebra cohomology H^*(𝔤; M) with coefficients in a 𝔤‑module M. The cochain space C^n(𝔤; M) = Hom(∧^n 𝔤, M) consists of alternating n‑linear maps. The differential d: C^n → Cn+1 is given by (dω)(X₀,…,X_n) = ∑i=0^n (−1)^i X_i·ω(X₀,…,̂X_i,…,X_n) + ∑0≤i<j≤n</sub> (−1)i+j ω([X_i,X_j], X₀,…,̂X_i,…,̂X_j,…,X_n). One checks d²=0 using the Jacobi identity. The cohomology groups H^n(𝔤; M) = ker d / im d are functorial in both arguments.

When M = ℂ is the trivial module, H^*(𝔤; ℂ) is a graded‑commutative algebra isomorphic to the algebra of invariant polynomials on 𝔤 (the Weil algebra) modulo the ideal generated by positive‑degree invariants. For a semisimple 𝔤, Whitehead’s lemmas state H¹(𝔤; M) = H²(𝔤; M) = 0 for any finite‑dimensional module M. Consequently, every finite‑dimensional representation of a semisimple Lie algebra is completely reducible (Weyl’s theorem). The third cohomology H³(𝔤; ℂ) is one‑dimensional, generated by the Cartan 3‑form ω(X,Y,Z) = κ([X,Y],Z) where κ is the Killing form. This class corresponds to the generator of H³(G; ℝ) for the compact Lie group G with Lie algebra 𝔤.

Relative Lie algebra cohomology H^*(𝔤, 𝔨; M) for a subalgebra 𝔨 ⊂ 𝔤 is defined using the subcomplex of cochains that are 𝔨‑equivariant and horizontal (vanish when any argument lies in 𝔨). For a symmetric pair (𝔤, 𝔨) with Cartan involution θ, the relative cohomology H^*(𝔤, 𝔨; ℝ) computes the de Rham cohomology of the symmetric space G/K. In particular, H^*(𝔤, 𝔨; ℝ) ≅ (∧^* 𝔭^*)^K where 𝔭 is the −1 eigenspace of θ. This isomorphism is the algebraic foundation of Matsushima’s formula for the Betti numbers of locally symmetric spaces.

Kostant’s theorem describes H^*(𝔫; ℂ) for the nilradical 𝔫 of a Borel subalgebra 𝔟 = 𝔥 ⊕ 𝔫. As an 𝔥‑module, H^q(𝔫; ℂ) ≅ ⊕w∈W, ℓ(w)=q ℂw(ρ)−ρ, where the sum runs over Weyl group elements of length q and ℂ_μ denotes the one‑dimensional 𝔥‑module of weight μ. This result underlies the Borel–Weil–Bott theorem and the geometric construction of irreducible representations via sheaf cohomology on the flag variety G/B.

Geometry of Irreducible Symmetric Spaces and Cartan’s Classification

A Riemannian symmetric space is a connected Riemannian manifold M such that for each point p ∈ M there exists an isometry s_p with s_p(p)=p and (ds_p)_p = −Id. The group of isometries G = Isom(M)⁰ acts transitively, and the stabilizer K of a base point is a compact subgroup; M ≅ G/K. The Lie algebra 𝔤 of G admits a Cartan decomposition 𝔤 = 𝔨 ⊕ 𝔭 where 𝔨 = Lie(K) and 𝔭 ≅ T_pM. The bracket satisfies [𝔨,𝔨]⊂𝔨, [𝔨,𝔭]⊂𝔭, [𝔭,𝔭]⊂𝔨. The curvature tensor at p is R(X,Y)Z = −[[X,Y],Z] for X,Y,Z ∈ 𝔭. A symmetric space is irreducible if its holonomy representation on 𝔭 is irreducible; equivalently, 𝔤 is simple (or 𝔤 = 𝔨 ⊕ 𝔭 with 𝔨 simple and 𝔭 an irreducible 𝔨‑module).

Élie Cartan classified all irreducible symmetric spaces in 1926. The compact type (positive sectional curvature) corresponds to 𝔤 compact semisimple; the non‑compact type (negative sectional curvature) corresponds to 𝔤 non‑compact semisimple with 𝔨 a maximal compact subalgebra. The list falls into seven infinite families and twelve exceptional cases. The compact families are: (I) A_I = SU(p+q)/S(U(p)×U(q)) (Grassmannians), (II) B_I = SO(p+q)/SO(p)×SO(q) (real Grassmannians), (III) C_I = Sp(p+q)/Sp(p)×Sp(q) (quaternionic Grassmannians), (IV) D_I = SO(2n)/U(n) (oriented Grassmannians), (V) A_II = SU(n)/SO(n) (space of real structures), (VI) A_III = SU(2n)/Sp(n) (space of quaternionic structures), (VII) D_III = SO(2n)/U(n) (same as IV but different embedding). The non‑compact duals are obtained by replacing the compact numerator with its non‑compact real form (e.g., SL(p+q,ℝ)/SO(p,q)). The exceptional symmetric spaces involve the exceptional Lie groups E₆, E₇, E₈, F₄, G₂; for instance, E₆/(Sp(4)/±I) (compact) and E₆(−14)/Spin(10)·U(1) (non‑compact) are 32‑dimensional.

Each symmetric space carries a canonical G‑invariant metric (the Killing form restricted to 𝔭) and a G‑invariant complex structure when the isotropy representation admits a complex structure (Hermitian symmetric spaces). The Hermitian symmetric spaces are exactly those of types A_I, A_II, A_III, D_III, and the two exceptional spaces E₆/(Spin(10)·U(1)) and E₇/(E₆·U(1)). They are Kähler–Einstein and admit a bounded symmetric domain realization (Harish‑Chandra embedding). The rank of a symmetric space is the dimension of a maximal flat totally geodesic submanifold; it equals the dimension of a maximal abelian subspace 𝔞 ⊂ 𝔭. The restricted root system Σ(𝔤,𝔞) governs the geometry of geodesics and the spherical functions on G/K.

  • Confusing the compact and non‑compact duals of a symmetric space; remember that duality replaces the numerator group by its non‑compact real form while keeping the same denominator.
  • Assuming every symmetric space is Hermitian; only the seven families listed above admit a G‑invariant complex structure.
  • Misapplying Whitehead’s lemmas to infinite‑dimensional modules; they hold only for finite‑dimensional modules over semisimple Lie algebras.
  • Forgetting that the Peter–Weyl theorem requires compactness; for non‑compact groups the decomposition involves a direct integral over the unitary dual.
  • Mixing up the Chevalley–Eilenberg differential for Lie algebra cohomology with the de Rham differential on the group manifold; they coincide only after identifying left‑invariant forms with the cochain complex.
  • Overlooking the role of the Weyl group length in Kostant’s theorem; the degree q equals the length ℓ(w) of the Weyl group element.
  • Treating the Cartan matrix as symmetric; it is symmetrizable but not symmetric unless all roots have the same length.

These advanced topics complete the structural and analytic toolkit needed for research‑level problems in Lie theory and its applications.

Representation Theory of Real Reductive Groups and the Langlands Classification

The representation theory of real reductive Lie groups extends the compact theory by incorporating non‑compact factors such as SL(2,ℝ), Sp(2n,ℝ), and the exceptional groups E₆(−26), E₇(−25). A real reductive group G has a Cartan decomposition G = K A⁺ K with K a maximal compact subgroup and A⁺ a closed Weyl chamber in a split torus. Irreducible admissible representations are classified by the Langlands parameters: a homomorphism from the Weil group W_ℝ into the L‑group ^L G modulo conjugation. For G = GL(n,ℝ) this recovers the classical correspondence between irreducible admissible representations and n‑dimensional representations of W_ℝ. The classification proceeds via the Langlands quotient construction: given a parabolic subgroup P = MAN and a tempered representation σ of M, one forms the normalized induced representation Ind_P^G(σ ⊗ ν ⊗ 1) with ν ∈ 𝔞*_ℂ. The unique irreducible quotient J(P,σ,ν) is the Langlands quotient. The tempered spectrum is described by the Plancherel formula of Harish‑Chandra, which expresses the regular representation on L²(G) as a direct integral over the tempered dual with the Plancherel measure μ(π). The Knapp–Stein R‑group governs the reducibility of induced representations; for GL(n) it is trivial, but for classical groups it can be a finite abelian group of order 2. The local Langlands correspondence for GL(n) was proved by Harris–Taylor and Henniart (2000); for other groups it remains a deep conjecture, now partially resolved by the work of Arthur on the endoscopic classification for symplectic and orthogonal groups.

Quantum Groups and Deformation Quantization

Quantum groups arise as Hopf algebra deformations of the universal enveloping algebra U(𝔤) of a semisimple Lie algebra 𝔤. The Drinfeld–Jimbo quantum group U_q(𝔤) depends on a parameter q ∈ ℂ^× (not a root of unity) and is generated by E_i, F_i, K_i±1 satisfying the q‑Serre relations. As q → 1 one recovers U(𝔤). The category of finite‑dimensional type‑1 representations of U_q(𝔤) is a braided monoidal category equivalent to the category of representations of the corresponding quantum affine algebra at generic q. The R‑matrix R ∈ U_q(𝔤) ⊗ U_q(𝔤) satisfies the quantum Yang–Baxter equation and provides braiding isomorphisms cV,W: V ⊗ W → W ⊗ V. At roots of unity the representation theory changes dramatically: the small quantum group u_q(𝔤) is finite‑dimensional and its representation category is not semisimple, leading to modular tensor categories used in topological quantum field theory. Deformation quantization, introduced by Bayen, Flato, Fronsdal, Lichnerowicz, and Sternheimer (1978), constructs a star‑product f ⋆ g = fg + (iℏ/2){f,g} + O(ℏ²) on the algebra of smooth functions on a Poisson manifold. For a symplectic manifold the Moyal–Weyl product gives a formal deformation; for a Lie–Poisson structure on 𝔤* the Gutt star‑product corresponds to the Duflo isomorphism. Kontsevich’s formality theorem (1997) proves that every Poisson manifold admits a deformation quantization, and the equivalence classes are classified by the second Poisson cohomology. In the context of quantum groups, the Drinfeld twist provides an explicit star‑product on the dual Hopf algebra Fun_q(G), linking quantum group symmetry to non‑commutative geometry.

Worked Example: Computing the R‑matrix for U_q(sl₂)
Let 𝔤 = sl₂ with standard generators e, f, h. The quantum group U_q(sl₂) has generators E, F, K±1 with relations K E K-1 = q² E, K F K-1 = q-2 F, [E,F] = (K - K-1)/(q - q-1). Write the universal R‑matrix as R = qh⊗h/2 ∑n=0^∞ (1 - q-2)^n / [n]_q! qn(n-1)/2 E^n ⊗ F^n, where [n]_q! = [1]_q [2]_q … [n]_q and [k]_q = (q^k - q-k)/(q - q-1). Verify that R satisfies (Δ ⊗ id)(R) = R13 R23 and the quantum Yang–Baxter equation R12 R13 R23 = R23 R13 R12.
Step 1: Recall the coproduct Δ(E) = E ⊗ K + 1 ⊗ E, Δ(F) = F ⊗ 1 + K-1 ⊗ F, Δ(K) = K ⊗ K. Step 2: Compute (Δ ⊗ id)(R) using the factorization R = qh⊗h/2 Θ with Θ = ∑n≥0 c_n E^n ⊗ F^n. Because Δ(qh/2) = qh/2 ⊗ qh/2, the Cartan part yields qh⊗h/2 ⊗ qh/2 = R13 R23 after identifying tensor legs. Step 3: For the nilpotent part, use the q‑binomial theorem to show (Δ ⊗ id)(Θ) = Θ13 Θ23. The coefficients match because the q‑exponential satisfies the same coproduct identity as the classical exponential. Step 4: The quantum Yang–Baxter equation follows from the coassociativity of Δ and the fact that R intertwines Δ and Δop: R Δ(x) = Δop(x) R for all x. This is a standard theorem; explicit verification for sl₂ reduces to checking the defining relations of U_q(sl₂) on the tensor product of three fundamental representations, which holds by construction of the R‑matrix.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the universal R‑matrix of a quantum group, write the factorized form qh⊗h/2 Θ and state the q‑exponential series for Θ. Do not attempt to sum the series; the closed form is the answer. For deformation quantization, remember that the star‑product is associative order by order in ℏ and that the first order term is (iℏ/2) times the Poisson bracket. Cite Kontsevich’s theorem only when existence for arbitrary Poisson manifolds is required.

Infinite-Dimensional Lie Algebras: Kac–Moody and Virasoro

Kac–Moody algebras generalize finite‑dimensional semisimple Lie algebras by allowing a generalized Cartan matrix A = (aij) of size n × n that is symmetrizable but not necessarily positive definite. The algebra 𝔤(A) is generated by e_i, f_i, h_i (i = 1,…,n) with Serre relations determined by A. If A is of finite type the algebra is finite‑dimensional semisimple; if A is of affine type (positive semidefinite, corank 1) one obtains an affine Kac–Moody algebra, a central extension of the loop algebra 𝔤 ⊗ ℂ[t,t-1] ⊕ ℂc ⊕ ℂd. The Virasoro algebra is the unique non‑trivial central extension of the Witt algebra of vector fields on the circle: it has basis L_n (n ∈ ℤ) and central element c with [L_m, L_n] = (m-n)Lm+n + (c/12)(m³-m)δm+n,0. Highest weight modules V(c,h) are generated by a vector v with L0 v = h v, L_n v = 0 (n>0). The Kac determinant formula gives the determinant of the Shapovalov form at each grade; zeros correspond to singular vectors and determine the structure of Verma modules. For affine algebras the Sugawara construction expresses the Virasoro generators as quadratic combinations of the affine currents: L_n = (1/2(k+h^∨)) ∑m∈ℤ :J^an-m J^a_m: where k is the level and h^∨ the dual Coxeter number. This yields a representation of the Virasoro algebra with central charge c = k dim 𝔤 / (k + h^∨). The characters of integrable highest weight modules are modular forms; the Weyl–Kac character formula generalizes the Weyl character formula and involves the affine Weyl group. These algebras underlie two‑dimensional conformal field theory, string theory, and the geometric Langlands program.

Geometric Quantization, the Orbit Method, and Moment Maps

Geometric quantization associates a Hilbert space to a symplectic manifold (M,ω) equipped with a prequantum line bundle L → M whose curvature is -iω. A polarization P (a Lagrangian integrable distribution) selects a subspace of covariantly constant sections; the quantum Hilbert space is H = {s ∈ Γ(L) | ∇_X s = 0 ∀ X ∈ P}. For a compact Lie group G acting Hamiltonianly on M with moment map μ: M → 𝔤*, the Marsden–Weinstein reduction M//G = μ-1(0)/G produces a reduced symplectic manifold. The Guillemin–Sternberg “quantization commutes with reduction” theorem states that the G‑invariant part of the quantization of M equals the quantization of M//G. The orbit method of Kirillov (1962) and Kostant (1970) constructs irreducible unitary representations of a nilpotent or solvable Lie group G from coadjoint orbits 𝒪_λ ⊂ 𝔤*. For a regular element λ, the orbit carries a canonical symplectic form ω_λ(X,Y) = λ([X,Y]); geometric quantization of 𝒪_λ with a suitable polarization yields the irreducible representation π_λ. For semisimple groups the method extends to the discrete series: Harish‑Chandra’s parametrization identifies discrete series representations with coadjoint orbits of elliptic elements. The Duistermaat–Heckman formula computes the pushforward of the Liouville measure under the moment map as a sum over fixed points of a torus action, providing exact stationary phase integrals. In the context of loop groups, the orbit method on the affine coadjoint orbits produces the positive energy representations of the Virasoro algebra and affine Kac–Moody algebras.

Harmonic Analysis on Non-Compact Groups: Plancherel Formula and Spherical Functions

For a non‑compact semisimple Lie group G with maximal compact subgroup K, the Plancherel theorem decomposes the regular representation on L²(G) into a direct integral of irreducible unitary representations. Harish‑Chandra’s Plancherel formula writes f(e) = ∫Ĝtemp f(π) dμ(π) for f ∈ C_c^∞(G), where Ĝtemp is the tempered dual and μ the Plancherel measure. The measure is supported on the principal series representations Ind_P^G(σ ⊗ ν) with ν ∈ i𝔞* and on the discrete series when they exist. Spherical functions φ_λ(g) = ∫_K e(iλ+ρ)(H(gk)) dk are joint eigenfunctions of the algebra 𝔻(G/K) of G‑invariant differential operators on the symmetric space G/K. They satisfy the Harish‑Chandra expansion φ_λ(exp H) = ∑w∈W c(wλ) e(i wλ - ρ)(H) with the c‑function c(λ) = ∏α>0 Γ(i⟨λ,α⟩) / Γ(i⟨λ,α⟩ + m_α/2 + m2α). The spherical transform tilde{f}(λ) = ∫_G f(g) φ-λ(g) dg diagonalizes the convolution algebra of K‑bi‑invariant functions; the inversion formula involves the Plancherel density |c(λ)|-2. For rank‑one groups the spherical functions are hypergeometric functions; for higher rank they are Heckman–Opdam hypergeometric functions associated with the restricted root system. The Paley–Wiener theorem characterizes the image of the spherical transform of compactly supported K‑bi‑invariant functions as entire functions of exponential type satisfying certain symmetry conditions. These tools are essential for the trace formula, automorphic forms, and the analytic continuation of Eisenstein series.

  • Confusing the Langlands quotient with the full induced representation; the quotient is irreducible but the induced module may have length >1.
  • Assuming the quantum R‑matrix is symmetric; it satisfies R21 R = 1 ⊗ 1 only after applying the antipode.
  • Treating the Virasoro central charge as a free parameter in all representations; for unitary highest weight modules it is constrained to c ≥ 1 or the discrete series c = 1 - 6/(m(m+1)).
  • Neglecting the metaplectic correction in geometric quantization; half‑form bundles are required for the correct inner product on the quantum Hilbert space.
  • Misapplying the Harish‑Chandra c‑function formula to non‑spherical representations; it is valid only for K‑bi‑invariant spherical functions.
  • Overlooking the distinction between the Plancherel measure for the full group and the spherical Plancherel measure for the Gelfand pair (G,K).
  • Forgetting that the orbit method for semisimple groups requires the orbit to be integral and the polarization to be compatible with the complex structure.

Lie Groupoids, Lie Algebroids, and the Integration Problem

A Lie groupoid generalizes both a Lie group and a smooth manifold: it consists of a manifold of arrows G and a manifold of objects M with source and target maps s, t : G → M, a unit map u : M → G, an inversion i : G → G, and a partially defined multiplication m : Gs ×t G → G satisfying associativity, unit, and inverse axioms. The Lie algebroid A = ker(ds)|M is a vector bundle over M equipped with an anchor map ρ : A → TM and a Lie bracket on sections satisfying the Leibniz rule [X, fY] = f[X,Y] + ρ(X)(f)Y. Every Lie groupoid differentiates to a Lie algebroid, but the converse — Lie's third theorem for algebroids — fails in general. The obstruction is measured by the monodromy groups Nx(A) ⊂ Ax introduced by Crainic and Fernandes (2003): a Lie algebroid integrates to a Lie groupoid iff its monodromy groups are uniformly discrete. When integration exists, the Weinstein groupoid G(A) is the unique source-simply-connected integration; any other integration is a quotient by a discrete central subgroupoid. For a Poisson manifold (M, π), the cotangent bundle T*M carries a Lie algebroid structure with anchor π# : T*M → TM and bracket [α, β] = Lπ#αβ − Lπ#βα − d(π(α, β)). Its integration yields the symplectic groupoid of M, which provides the geometric framework for deformation quantization. The moduli stack of Lie groupoids with fixed Lie algebroid is controlled by the cohomology H2(A, ℤ) of the algebroid, and the Morita equivalence of Lie groupoids corresponds to the equivalence of their differentiable stacks. The Connes–Thom isomorphism in KK-theory relates the K-theory of the convolution algebra Cc∞(G) to the K-theory of the base M when G is a foliation groupoid, linking Lie groupoid theory to the Baum–Connes conjecture.

Geometric Representation Theory: Perverse Sheaves, the Springer Correspondence, and Character Sheaves

The Springer correspondence (1976) constructs irreducible representations of the Weyl group W from the topology of the Springer resolution μ : T*G/B → N, where N ⊂ 𝔤 is the nilpotent cone and G/B the flag variety. The fibers μ−1(x) are the Springer fibers Bx; their cohomology H•(Bx, ℚℓ) carries a natural W-action, and the top cohomology Htop(Bx) decomposes as a sum of irreducible W-representations indexed by pairs (x, φ) where x ∈ N and φ is an irreducible representation of the component group A(x) = ZG(x)/ZG(x)0. The decomposition theorem of Beilinson–Bernstein–Deligne–Gabber (1982) implies that the direct image μ*ℚℓ[dim N] splits as a direct sum of intersection cohomology complexes IC(Ō, ℒ) supported on nilpotent orbit closures Ō with local systems ℒ. This identifies the Springer correspondence with the Kazhdan–Lusztig classification of simple perverse sheaves on N. Lusztig's character sheaves (1985–1992) extend this to the group G itself: a character sheaf on G is a G-equivariant perverse sheaf on G (for the conjugation action) whose restriction to each conjugacy class is a local system placed in the appropriate degree. The set of character sheaves is partitioned into families indexed by unipotent classes, and their characteristic functions on G(Fq) form an orthonormal basis of the space of class functions, generalizing the Green functions. The geometric Satake equivalence (Mirković–Vilonen, 2007) identifies the category of G-equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian GrG = G(K)/G(O) with the category of finite-dimensional representations of the Langlands dual group Ǧ. The convolution product on perverse sheaves corresponds to the tensor product of representations, and the fiber functor is given by the global cohomology. This provides a geometric realization of the tensor category Rep(Ǧ) and underlies the geometric Langlands program. The Beilinson–Drinfeld Grassmannian over a curve X globalizes this to a factorization algebra, and the Hecke eigensheaf property characterizes automorphic D-modules on BunG(X).

Worked Problem: Springer Fiber Cohomology for SL(3)
Let G = SL(3, ℂ) with Lie algebra 𝔰𝔩(3). The nilpotent cone N has three orbits: {0}, the minimal orbit O_min of matrices of rank 1, and the regular orbit O_reg of matrices of rank 2. For x ∈ O_min, compute the Betti numbers of the Springer fiber B_x and identify the W-representation on H^•(B_x).
For SL(3), the Weyl group W ≅ S3 has three irreducible representations: trivial 1, sign ε, and standard 2-dimensional ρ. The Springer fiber B_x for x ∈ O_min is isomorphic to ℙ1. Its cohomology is H0 ≅ ℚ (trivial), H2 ≅ ℚ (trivial), and H1 = 0. The W-action on H0 ⊕ H2 is the sum of two trivial representations. However, the Springer correspondence assigns to the pair (O_min, trivial local system) the representation ρ ⊗ ε (which is ρ since ρ ≅ ρ ⊗ ε for S3). The top cohomology H2(B_x) carries the trivial representation, but the full graded representation H^•(B_x) = H0 ⊕ H2 is the trivial representation with multiplicity 2. The correct statement: the Springer correspondence matches (O_reg, trivial) 1, (O_min, trivial) ρ, ({0}, trivial) ε. The graded W-module H^•(B_x) for x ∈ O_min is 1 ⊕ 1, while the irreducible constituent in top degree is 1. The representation ρ appears in the cohomology of the Steinberg variety Z = μ-1(0) ×_N μ-1(0) via the convolution action.
Exam Strategy: Geometric Representation Theory
Memorize the three pillars: (1) Springer correspondence: nilpotent orbits + local systems irreducible W-representations. (2) Geometric Satake: perverse sheaves on affine Grassmannian Rep(Ǧ). (3) Character sheaves: G-equivariant perverse sheaves on G class functions on G(F_q). For multiple-choice questions, the key distinction is that Springer fibers give W-representations, while the affine Grassmannian gives Ǧ-representations. The decomposition theorem is the engine behind both. If asked about the field of coefficients, the ℓ-adic setting (ℚ_ℓ) is standard for finite fields; the complex setting uses constructible sheaves on complex varieties. The IC-sheaf IC(Ō, ℒ) is the unique simple perverse sheaf extending ℒ[dim O] from the open orbit O.

The Local Langlands Correspondence for General Linear Groups

The local Langlands correspondence (LLC) for GL(n, F) over a non-archimedean local field F establishes a bijection between the set of irreducible smooth complex representations of GL(n, F) and the set of n-dimensional Frobenius-semisimple Weil–Deligne representations of the Weil group WF. A Weil–Deligne representation is a pair (ρ, N) where ρ : WF → GL(n, ℂ) is a representation with open kernel and N ∈ End(ℂ^n) is nilpotent satisfying ρ(w) N ρ(w)−1 = ‖w‖ N for all w ∈ WF. The correspondence π (ρ, N) is characterized by the preservation of L-functions and ε-factors of pairs: for any irreducible smooth representation π' of GL(m, F), one requires L(s, π × π') = L(s, (ρ, N) ⊗ (ρ', N')) and similarly for ε-factors. The proof for GL(n) was completed independently by Harris–Taylor (2001) and Henniart (2000). Harris–Taylor used the Shimura variety for GL(n) (the Lubin–Tate tower) and the Kottwitz isomorphism to compare the cohomology of the tower with the representation theory of GL(n) and its inner forms. Henniart's proof used global methods: embedding the local problem into a global automorphic representation and applying the Chebotarev density theorem with the trace formula. The LLC satisfies compatibility with parabolic induction: if π = IndPGL(n)(π1 ⊗ ⋯ ⊗ πr) corresponds to (ρ, N) = (ρ1, N1) ⊕ ⋯ ⊕ (ρr, Nr), then the correspondence respects the induction. For supercuspidal representations, the correspondence is constructed via Bushnell–Kutzko types and the Langlands parameter is built from the Weil group representation attached to the simple stratum. The local Langlands correspondence for GL(n) is the only case where a complete, explicit, and canonical bijection is known for all n. For other groups, the correspondence is conjectural (the Langlands parameterization of the tempered dual) or known only in special cases (e.g., SL(2) by Gelbart–Knapp, classical groups by Arthur). The local Langlands correspondence for GL(n) also underlies the modularity of Galois representations via the global Langlands correspondence and the Taylor–Wiles method.

Quantum Groups at Roots of Unity, Modular Tensor Categories, and Topological Quantum Field Theory

When the deformation parameter q is a primitive ℓ-th root of unity (ℓ odd, not divisible by 3 for G2), the quantum group Uq(𝔤) acquires a large center: the ℓ-center Zℓ generated by the ℓ-th powers of the divided powers Ei(ℓ), Fi(ℓ), and Kiℓ. The quotient uq(𝔤) = Uq(𝔤) / ⟨Zℓ − χ(Zℓ)⟩ is the small quantum group (or Frobenius–Lusztig kernel), a finite-dimensional Hopf algebra of dimension ℓdim 𝔤. Its representation category Rep(uq(𝔤)) is a modular tensor category (MTC) after semisimplification: one kills the negligible morphisms (those with zero quantum trace) to obtain a semisimple category with finitely many simple objects, a braiding cX,Y : X ⊗ Y → Y ⊗ X, a twist θX : X → X, and a non-degenerate S-matrix Si,j = tr(cj,i ci,j). The simple objects are indexed by the alcove Aℓ = {λ ∈ P+ | ⟨λ + ρ, θ⟩ < ℓ} where θ is the highest root. The Verlinde formula computes the fusion coefficients Ni,jk = ∑m Si,m Sj,m Sk,m* / S0,m. The MTC Rep(uq(𝔤))ss is equivalent to the category of integrable highest-weight modules of the affine Kac–Moody algebra ĝ at level k = ℓ − h∨ (where h∨ is the dual Coxeter number). This equivalence is the Kazhdan–Lusztig correspondence (1991–1994). The Reshetikhin–Turaev construction (1990) uses an MTC to build a 3-dimensional topological quantum field theory (TQFT): to a closed oriented surface Σ of genus g it assigns the vector space V(Σ) = Hom(1, (X ⊗ X*)⊗g) where X = ⊕i Vi is the sum of all simple objects; to a 3-manifold M with boundary Σ it assigns a vector Z(M) ∈ V(Σ) computed by surgery on a framed link in S3 using the Kirby color Ω = ∑i dimq(Vi) Vi. The Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of 3-manifolds are the partition functions Z(M) for closed M. For SU(2) at level k, the MTC is the Fibonacci category when k = 3 (simple objects 1, τ with τ ⊗ τ = 1 ⊕ τ) and the Ising category when k = 2. These MTCs describe anyonic statistics in topological quantum computation: the braiding matrices give unitary gates, and the non-abelian anyons (e.g., Fibonacci anyons) allow universal quantum computation. The Drinfeld center Z(C) of a fusion category C is a modular tensor category; for C = Rep(G) with G finite, Z(C) is the category of G-graded G-equivariant vector spaces, and the TQFT is the Dijkgraaf–Witten theory with gauge group G.

Lie Theory in Positive Characteristic: Algebraic Groups, Frobenius Kernels, and the Steinberg Tensor Product Theorem

Let G be a connected reductive algebraic group over an algebraically closed field k of characteristic p > 0. The Frobenius morphism F : G → G is the p-th power map on coordinates; its kernel G1 = ker F is the first Frobenius kernel, an infinitesimal group scheme of height 1. Its coordinate algebra k[G1] is the restricted enveloping algebra u(𝔤) of the Lie algebra 𝔤 = Lie(G), a finite-dimensional Hopf algebra of dimension pdim 𝔤. The higher Frobenius kernels Gr = ker Fr form a tower of normal subgroup schemes with Gr ⊂ Gr+1 and G = ∪r Gr (as ind-scheme). The category of rational G-modules is equivalent to the category of Gr-modules compatible with the restriction maps. The Steinberg tensor product theorem (1963) states that for any dominant weight λ ∈ X(T)+, write its p-adic expansion λ = λ0 + p λ1 + p2 λ2 + ⋯ with 0 ≤ ⟨λi, α∨⟩ < p for all simple coroots α∨. Then the simple G-module L(λ) decomposes as a tensor product L(λ) ≅ L(λ0) ⊗ L(λ1)(1) ⊗ L(λ2)(2) ⊗ ⋯ where (i) denotes the i-th Frobenius twist. The factors L(λi)(i) are simple modules for the Frobenius kernel Gi+1 and are restricted (i.e., their weights lie in the fundamental alcove). The linkage principle (Andersen–Jantzen–Soergel, 1994) describes the block structure: two simple modules L(λ) and L(μ) lie in the same block iff λ and μ are linked under the affine Weyl group Wp = W ⋉ pℤΦ acting by w · λ = w(λ + ρ) − ρ (the p-dilated dot action). The Lusztig conjecture (proved by Andersen–Jantzen–Soergel for large p and by Riche–Williamson for all p ≥ h via the geometric Satake equivalence in positive characteristic) gives the characters of simple modules L(λ) for λ in the lowest p-alcove in terms of Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials evaluated at 1. The cohomology of line bundles on the flag variety G/B is computed by the Kempf vanishing theorem: Hi(G/B, ℒ(λ)) = 0 for i > 0 and λ dominant; for arbitrary λ, the cohomology is given by the Borel–Weil–Bott theorem in characteristic p which fails in general but holds for λ in the Jantzen region. The support varieties for Gr-modules are subvarieties of the nullcone N1(𝔤) = {x ∈ 𝔤 | x[p] = 0} and detect complexity and periodicity. The tilting modules T(λ) (modules with both a Weyl filtration and a good filtration) form a category equivalent to the category of Soergel bimodules for the affine Weyl group, providing a categorification of the Hecke algebra.

  • Confusing the small quantum group uq(𝔤) with the restricted enveloping algebra u(𝔤) in characteristic p; they are analogous but live in different settings (root of unity vs. positive characteristic).
  • Assuming the Springer correspondence works identically in positive characteristic; the characteristic p Springer correspondence involves the affine Weyl group and perverse sheaves on the affine flag variety.
  • Treating the Langlands dual group Ǧ as the same as the original group G for simply-laced types; the dual group has the dual root system, which coincides only for simply-laced types.
  • Neglecting the Frobenius twist in the Steinberg tensor product theorem; the factor L(λi)(i) is not a G-module but a Gi+1-module pulled back via Fi.
  • Confusing the modular tensor category from quantum groups at roots of unity with the category of representations of the affine Kac–Moody algebra; they are equivalent as braided tensor categories but the equivalence is non-trivial (Kazhdan–Lusztig).
  • Assuming the local Langlands correspondence for GL(n) extends directly to other groups; for non-GL groups the correspondence is a parameterization of the tempered dual by Langlands parameters, not a bijection with all irreducible representations.
  • Overlooking the role of the ℓ-center in the representation theory of Uq(𝔤) at roots of unity; the central characters determine the blocks, and the small quantum group is the fiber over a central character.

These advanced topics form the frontier where Lie theory meets geometric representation theory, number theory, and topological quantum field theory.

Lie Superalgebras and Supergroups: Graded Geometry and Representation Theory

A Lie superalgebra is a ℤ₂-graded vector space 𝔤 = 𝔤₀ ⊕ 𝔤₁ equipped with a bilinear bracket [·,·] satisfying graded skew-symmetry [x,y] = −(−1)|x||y|[y,x] and the graded Jacobi identity [x,[y,z]] = [[x,y],z] + (−1)|x||y|[y,[x,z]] for homogeneous elements. The even part 𝔤₀ is an ordinary Lie algebra; the odd part 𝔤₁ is a 𝔤₀-module. The basic classical simple Lie superalgebras over ℂ were classified by Kac (1977): the series A(m,n) = 𝔰𝔩(m+1|n+1) (m ≠ n), B(m,n) = 𝔬𝔰𝔭(2m+1|2n), C(n) = 𝔬𝔰𝔭(2|2n−2), D(m,n) = 𝔬𝔰𝔭(2m|2n), and the exceptional types D(2,1;α), F(4), G(3). The strange series P(n) and Q(n) complete the list. A Lie supergroup is a group object in the category of supermanifolds; its tangent space at the identity is a Lie superalgebra. The Harish-Chandra pair (G₀, 𝔤) consists of an ordinary Lie group G₀ with Lie algebra 𝔤₀ and a Lie superalgebra 𝔤 with even part 𝔤₀, together with an action of G₀ on 𝔤 extending the adjoint action. Finite-dimensional irreducible representations of basic classical Lie superalgebras are highest-weight modules L(λ) parameterized by dominant integral weights λ, but the category is not semisimple: atypical blocks contain indecomposable projectives with Loewy length > 1. The Kac–Wakimoto character formula expresses the character of a finite-dimensional irreducible module as a quotient of alternating sums over the Weyl group, with a denominator that vanishes on atypical weights. The Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand category 𝒪 for Lie superalgebras has enough projectives and injectives; the BGG reciprocity holds, but the Jantzen filtration and translation functors require care due to the non-semisimplicity of the even subalgebra action on odd root spaces. The superdimension sdim V = dim V₀ − dim V₁ replaces dimension in many formulas; the supertrace str(x) = tr(x|V₀) − tr(x|V₁) gives the character of the adjoint representation. The Casimir element Ω = ∑ x_i x^i (with dual basis relative to the Killing form) acts by a scalar on irreducibles, but the Killing form is degenerate for types A(m,m), P(n), Q(n), requiring the use of a non-degenerate invariant supersymmetric bilinear form. The representation theory of the general linear Lie superalgebra 𝔤𝔩(m|n) is governed by the Sergeev duality with the queer Lie superalgebra 𝔮(n), and the Schur–Weyl duality for (ℂm|n)⊗k involves the Hecke algebra at q = −1. The cohomology of Lie superalgebras with coefficients in a module is computed by the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex with graded symmetric algebra on the odd part; the cohomology ring H*(𝔤, ℂ) is a finitely generated supercommutative algebra. The geometry of flag supervarieties G/B for supergroups involves the Borel subgroup B with Lie superalgebra 𝔟 = 𝔥 ⊕ 𝔫; the Borel–Weil–Bott theorem holds for dominant weights but fails for atypical weights, where higher cohomology does not vanish. The supergroup GL(m|n) acts on the super Grassmannian Gr(r|s; m|n); its coordinate ring is a quantum superalgebra at q = 1.

Quantum Affine Algebras, Crystal Bases, and the Combinatorics of Representations

The quantum affine algebra U_q(ĝ) is the Drinfeld–Jimbo quantization of the universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra ĝ = 𝔤 ⊗ ℂ[t,t⁻¹] ⊕ ℂc ⊕ ℂd. It has generators e_i, f_i, k_i±1 for i = 0,…,r (r = rank 𝔤) with relations deforming the Serre relations of ĝ. The Drinfeld realization provides an alternative presentation with currents x_i^±(z), φ_i^±(z), k_i±1, capturing the loop structure. Finite-dimensional irreducible representations of U_q(ĝ) are classified by Drinfeld polynomials P_i(u) ∈ ℂ[u] (i = 1,…,r) with P_i(0) = 1; the highest weight is encoded in the rational functions q_i(u) = P_i(uq_i-1)/P_i(uq_i) where q_i = qd_i and d_i are the symmetrizing integers. The tensor product of two such representations is irreducible iff the Drinfeld polynomials satisfy a certain coprimality condition (the Chari–Pressley criterion). The category of finite-dimensional representations is not braided, but the R-matrix provides a commutor satisfying the Yang–Baxter equation. Crystal bases, introduced by Kashiwara (1990), are combinatorial skeletons of integrable highest-weight modules at q = 0. For U_q(ĝ), the crystal B(λ) of a highest-weight module L(λ) is a set with operators ẽ_i, f̃_i (i = 0,…,r) and weight function wt: B → P (the weight lattice) satisfying the crystal axioms. The crystal graph is a colored directed graph; connected components correspond to irreducible summands. The tensor product rule for crystals is given by the signature rule: for b₁ ⊗ b₂, the operator f̃_i acts on the leftmost factor where the i-signature has an unpaired −. The global crystal basis (canonical basis) of L(λ) is a ℤ[q]-basis invariant under the bar involution and congruent to the crystal basis modulo q. The character of L(λ) is the sum over b ∈ B(λ) of qenergy(b) ewt(b) where energy is a grading function. The Kirillov–Reshetikhin modules W(a)_m are finite-dimensional representations associated to a node a of the Dynkin diagram and a positive integer m; their characters satisfy the Q-system (a system of quadratic relations) and the T-system (a system of relations in the Grothendieck ring). The fermionic formula expresses the q-multiplicity of a weight in a tensor product of Kirillov–Reshetikhin modules as a sum over rigged configurations, providing a combinatorial interpretation of the Kostka–Foulkes polynomials. The geometric realization of crystals via quiver varieties (Nakajima) and the geometric Satake equivalence for affine Grassmannians (Mirković–Vilonen) give a geometric construction of the canonical basis and the tensor product multiplicities. The quantum affine algebra at roots of unity yields the small quantum affine algebra, whose representation theory is controlled by the restricted quantum group and the Frobenius map to the classical affine algebra.

Worked Example: Crystal Graph of the Vector Representation of U_q(ŝ𝔩₂)
Let U_q(ŝ𝔩₂) be the quantum affine algebra of type A₁⁽¹⁾ with nodes 0,1. The vector representation V = L(Λ₀) has highest weight Λ₀ (fundamental weight for node 0). Draw the crystal graph B(Λ₀) with vertices labeled by weights and edges labeled by ẽ₀, f̃₀, ẽ₁, f̃₁. Compute the action of f̃₀ and f̃₁ on the highest weight vector v₀.
The crystal B(Λ₀) for U_q(ŝ𝔩₂) has four vertices corresponding to the weights Λ₀, Λ₀ − α₀, Λ₀ − α₀ − α₁, Λ₀ − α₁ (where α₀ = δ − α, α₁ = α). The highest weight vector v₀ has weight Λ₀. The operators act as: f̃₀(v₀) = v₁ (weight Λ₀ − α₀), f̃₁(v₀) = 0. Then f̃₁(v₁) = v₂ (weight Λ₀ − α₀ − α₁), f̃₀(v₁) = 0. Next f̃₀(v₂) = v₃ (weight Λ₀ − α₁), f̃₁(v₂) = 0. Finally f̃₁(v₃) = 0, f̃₀(v₃) = 0. The ẽ_i operators reverse these arrows. The crystal graph is a path of length 3: v₀ → v₁ → v₂ → v₃ with alternating colors 0,1,0. The character is ch V = eΛ₀ + eΛ₀−α₀ + eΛ₀−α₀−α₁ + eΛ₀−α₁. The Drinfeld polynomial is P₁(u) = 1 + u (for node 1, the finite node), giving q₁(u) = (1+uq)/(1+u). The R-matrix on V ⊗ V satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation and gives the six-vertex model.
Exam Strategy: Crystal Bases and Drinfeld Polynomials
When asked to compute a crystal graph, first identify the highest weight and the Dynkin diagram. Draw the weight lattice and apply f̃_i using the signature rule: write the sequence of ε_i(b) and φ_i(b) values, pair + and −, and f̃_i acts on the leftmost unpaired −. For Drinfeld polynomials, remember that P_i(u) = ∏ (1 + u/a_j) where a_j are the spectral parameters; the highest weight is recovered from q_i(u) = P_i(uq_i-1)/P_i(uq_i). The tensor product of two Kirillov–Reshetikhin modules W(a)_m ⊗ W(b)_n is irreducible iff the Drinfeld polynomials are coprime. The Q-system for type A_r is [W(a)_m]² = [W(a)m-1][W(a)m+1] + [W(a-1)_m][W(a+1)_m] (with boundary conditions). Memorize the fermionic formula for the q-multiplicity: it is a sum over rigged configurations with energy given by a quadratic form. In multiple-choice questions, the crystal graph of the vector representation of U_q(ŝ𝔩_n) is always a path of length n with alternating colors; the adjoint representation crystal has a more complex structure with loops.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence: D-Modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles

The geometric Langlands conjecture, formulated by Drinfeld and Laumon (1990s) and developed by Beilinson–Drinfeld, Gaitsgory, and others, is a geometric analogue of the Langlands correspondence for function fields. Let X be a smooth projective curve over ℂ, G a reductive algebraic group, and Ǧ its Langlands dual. The moduli stack Bun_G(X) of G-bundles on X is an algebraic stack of infinite type. The category of D-modules on Bun_G(X) (or ind-coherent sheaves, in the derived setting) is the automorphic side. The spectral side is the category of quasi-coherent sheaves (or ind-coherent sheaves) on the stack LocSys_Ǧ(X) of Ǧ-local systems on X (i.e., Ǧ-bundles with flat connection). The geometric Langlands correspondence predicts an equivalence of derived categories D-mod(Bun_G(X)) ≃ QCoh(LocSys_Ǧ(X)) (or IndCoh on the de Rham stack). The Hecke operators H_x: D-mod(Bun_G) → D-mod(Bun_G × X) for x ∈ X act by modifying the G-bundle at x; they are integral transforms with kernels given by the Springer resolution. A Hecke eigensheaf is a D-module F on Bun_G equipped with isomorphisms H_x(F) ≃ F ⊠ E_x where E_x is a Ǧ-local system on X; the collection {E_x} glues to a Ǧ-local system E on X, the eigenvalue. The conjecture states that irreducible Hecke eigensheaves correspond to irreducible Ǧ-local systems. The abelian case G = GL₁ was proved by Rothstein (1996) and Laumon (1996) using Fourier–Mukai transform on the Picard stack. For GL_n, the correspondence was proved by Laumon (1996) for n=2 and by Drinfeld (2000s) for general n using the geometric Satake equivalence and the theory of perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian Gr_G = G(ℂ((t)))/G(ℂ[[t]]). The geometric Satake equivalence (Mirković–Vilonen, Ginzburg) identifies Rep(Ǧ) with the category of G(ℂ[[t]])-equivariant perverse sheaves on Gr_G; the convolution product corresponds to tensor product of representations. The Whittaker model for D-modules on Bun_G involves the Whittaker sheaf on the stack of G-bundles with a reduction to the unipotent radical; the geometric Langlands correspondence intertwines the Whittaker functor with the fiber functor at the trivial local system. The categorical geometric Langlands (Gaitsgory–Lurie, 2019) proves an equivalence of ∞-categories IndCoh_Nilp(LocSys_Ǧ) ≃ D-mod(Bun_G) for any reductive G, using the theory of spectral gluing and the nilpotent singular support condition. The ramified geometric Langlands allows tame ramification at marked points; the local geometric Langlands for the formal disc relates D-modules on the affine flag variety to representations of the affine Hecke algebra. The connection to mirror symmetry: the moduli space of Higgs bundles M_H(G) is a hyperkähler manifold; the geometric Langlands correspondence is conjecturally the Fourier–Mukai transform on the Hitchin fibration M_H(G) → A (the Hitchin base), exchanging branes of type (B,A,A) and (A,B,A) in the sense of Kapustin–Witten. The quantum geometric Langlands introduces a parameter ℏ (or q = eℏ) and replaces D-modules with modules over the sheaf of quantum differential operators; it is related to the representation theory of quantum groups at roots of unity and to 4D N=4 super Yang–Mills theory with a surface operator.

Cluster Algebras, Quiver Varieties, and the Categorification of Quantum Groups

Cluster algebras, introduced by Fomin and Zelevinsky (2002), are commutative algebras generated by clusters of variables related by mutations. A seed consists of a quiver Q (or a skew-symmetrizable matrix B) and a cluster (x₁,…,x_n); mutation at vertex k produces a new quiver μ_k(Q) and a new cluster where x_k is replaced by x_k' = (∏i→k x_i + ∏k→j x_j)/x_k. The cluster algebra A(Q) is the ℤ-subalgebra of the field of rational functions generated by all cluster variables. The Laurent phenomenon states that every cluster variable is a Laurent polynomial in any initial cluster. Cluster algebras of finite type are classified by Dynkin diagrams (ADE); the cluster variables correspond to almost positive roots, and the exchange graph is the graph of clusters. The cluster category C_Q (Buan–Marsh–Reineke–Reiten–Todorov) is the orbit category D^b(mod kQ)/τ⁻¹[1]; its indecomposable objects correspond to cluster variables, and clusters correspond to tilting objects. Quiver varieties, introduced by Nakajima (1994), provide a geometric construction of representations of Kac–Moody algebras. For a quiver Q with dimension vectors v, w, the quiver variety M(v,w) is a hyperkähler quotient of the representation space of the doubled quiver. The cohomology H*(M(v,w)) carries an action of the Kac–Moody algebra 𝔤_Q; the top cohomology gives the irreducible highest-weight module L(λ) with λ = w. The convolution algebra H_*BM(M(v,w) ×_A M(v',w)) (Borel–Moore homology) is isomorphic to the positive part U_q(𝔫) of the quantum group. The categorification of quantum groups via quiver varieties (Nakajima, Varagnolo–Vasserot) identifies the canonical basis with the classes of irreducible components of Lagrangian subvarieties. The cluster structure on the coordinate ring of the unipotent cell N (or the double Bruhat cell Gu,v) was established by Berenstein–Fomin–Zelevinsky; the cluster variables are generalized minors. The quantum cluster algebra has generators satisfying q-commutation relations; the quantum dilogarithm identities give the mutation formulas. The categorification of cluster algebras via 2-Calabi–Yau categories (Amiot, Keller) associates to a quiver with potential (Q,W) the Ginzburg dg-algebra Γ(Q,W); its derived category D_fd(Γ) is 2-Calabi–Yau and its cluster tilting objects correspond to clusters. The cluster character map sends objects of D_fd(Γ) to the quantum cluster algebra. The categorification of the quantum group U_q(𝔤) via Khovanov–Lauda–Rouquier (KLR) algebras (or quiver Hecke algebras) gives a graded algebra R(β) for each dimension vector β; the category of projective R(β)-modules categorifies U_q(𝔫⁻). The cyclotomic KLR algebras categorify highest-weight modules. The connection to Lie theory: the cluster algebra structure on the coordinate ring of the base affine space G/N is compatible with the Poisson structure; the quantum cluster algebra is a deformation quantization. The scattering diagram of Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich gives a wall-crossing formula for cluster mutations, related to the Kontsevich–Soibelman wall-crossing formula for Donaldson–Thomas invariants. The cluster ensemble (A,X) gives a pair of dual cluster varieties; the tropical points of the X-variety are the g-vectors, and the tropical points of the A-variety are the F-polynomials. The Fock–Goncharov conjecture (proved for skew-symmetric cases) states that the canonical basis of the quantum cluster algebra is given by the tropical points of the dual variety.

Representations of p-Adic Groups: Types, Covers, and the Local Langlands Correspondence Beyond GL(n)

Let F be a non-archimedean local field with residue field of cardinality q, and G a connected reductive group over F. The category of smooth complex representations of G(F) is the central object of the local Langlands program. A representation is admissible if the space of vectors fixed by any open compact subgroup is finite-dimensional. The Bernstein decomposition writes the category as a product over inertial equivalence classes of supercuspidal supports: Rep(G) = ∏_Ω Rep(G)^Ω. A type (J,λ) for a Bernstein component Ω is a pair of an open compact subgroup J ⊂ G(F) and an irreducible representation λ of J such that the functor Hom_J(λ, −) gives an equivalence between Rep(G)^Ω and modules over the Hecke algebra ℋ(G,λ). Bushnell–Kutzko (1993) constructed types for GL_n(F) using simple strata and the theory of simple characters; the Hecke algebra is an affine Hecke algebra of type A. For general groups, Yu (2001) constructed tame supercuspidal representations and types using generic cuspidal G-data (a sequence of twisted Levi subgroups, characters, and a generic character of a maximal unipotent subgroup). The Hecke algebra for a Yu type is a twisted affine Hecke algebra. The local Langlands correspondence for GL_n(F) (Harris–Taylor, Henniart, 2000) is a bijection between irreducible admissible representations of GL_n(F) and n-dimensional Frobenius-semisimple Weil–Deligne representations of the Weil group W_F, preserving L- and ε-factors. For general G, the correspondence is a finite-to-one map from the set of irreducible admissible representations of G(F) to the set of Langlands parameters φ: W_F × SL₂(ℂ) → Ǧ(ℂ) (up to Ǧ-conjugacy), where Ǧ is the Langlands dual group. The fiber over φ is the L-packet Π_φ; its elements are parameterized by irreducible representations of the component group S_φ = π₀(CentǦ(Im φ)/Z(Ǧ)Γ_F). The endoscopic classification (Arthur, 2013) for classical groups (symplectic, orthogonal) uses the stable trace formula to characterize L-packets in terms of endoscopic transfer. The local Langlands correspondence for inner forms of GL_n (Badulescu, 2008) uses the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence. The Kaletha (2019) construction of regular supercuspidal representations uses regular depth-zero and positive-depth data; the L-packets are singletons and the correspondence is explicit. The Fargues–Scholze (2021) geometrization of the local Langlands correspondence uses the moduli stack of G-bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve; the Hecke operators act on the category of sheaves on this stack, and the correspondence is realized as a spectral action of the category of representations of Ǧ. The Bushnell–Kutzko theory of types for GL_n extends to the pro-p Iwahori subgroup; the pro-p Iwahori–Hecke algebra (Vignéras) has a presentation by generators and relations, and its representation theory is related to the mod p Langlands correspondence. The mod p local Langlands correspondence for GL₂(ℚ_p) (Breuil, Colmez, Paskunas) relates smooth mod p representations to 2-dimensional Galois representations over 𝔽̄_p; the completed cohomology of modular curves gives a p-adic Banach space representation of GL₂(ℚ_p) × Gal(ℚ̄_p/ℚ_p). The categorical local Langlands (Ben-Zvi–Sakuma–Nadler) conjectures an equivalence between the category of D-modules on the stack of G-local systems on the punctured disc and the category of representations of the loop group G(ℂ((t))). The relative Langlands program studies periods of automorphic forms; the local relative trace formula (Jacquet, Ye, Zhang) gives a spectral decomposition of the space of functions on a symmetric space G(F)/H(F). The Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture (proved by Waldspurger, Beuzart-Plessis, etc.) relates the non-vanishing of periods to the central value of L-functions.

  • Confusing the crystal graph of a highest-weight module with the crystal of the corresponding quantum affine algebra representation; the latter has an extra node 0 and the operators ẽ₀, f̃₀ act non-trivially.
  • Assuming the geometric Langlands equivalence holds for D-modules on the coarse moduli space of G-bundles; it requires the stack Bun_G and the nilpotent singular support condition on the spectral side.
  • Treating the cluster algebra of a quiver as the same as the coordinate ring of the corresponding quiver variety; the cluster algebra is a subalgebra of the coordinate ring of the open cell, and the full coordinate ring requires frozen variables.
  • Believing the local Langlands correspondence for a general reductive group G is a bijection; it is a finite-to-one map with fibers (L-packets) parameterized by representations of the component group S_φ.
  • Confusing the Yu construction of tame supercuspidals (which works for all groups but requires tame ramification) with the Bushnell–Kutzko construction for GL_n (which works for all supercuspidals but is specific to GL_n).
  • Assuming the quantum cluster algebra is commutative; it is a non-commutative algebra with q-commutation relations, and the quantum dilogarithm governs the mutation.
  • Overlooking the role of the Frobenius map in the representation theory of quantum affine algebras at roots of unity; the small quantum affine algebra maps to the classical affine algebra, and the blocks are controlled by the restricted quantum group.

Borel–Weil–Bott Theorem and Line Bundles on Flag Varieties

The Borel–Weil–Bott theorem gives a complete description of the sheaf cohomology of homogeneous line bundles on the flag variety G/B of a complex semisimple Lie group G. Let λ be an integral weight, viewed as a character of the Borel subgroup B. The associated line bundle L(λ) on G/B has cohomology groups Hⁱ(G/B, L(λ)) that are either zero or an irreducible G-module with highest weight given by the dot action of the Weyl group. Concretely, if λ+ρ is singular (lies on a wall of the Weyl chambers) then all cohomology vanishes. If λ+ρ is regular, there is a unique element w in the Weyl group such that w·(λ+ρ) is dominant; then Hℓ(w)(G/B, L(λ)) is the irreducible representation V(w·λ) of highest weight w·λ = w(λ+ρ)−ρ, and all other cohomology groups vanish. The length ℓ(w) equals the number of positive roots sent to negative roots by w. This theorem realises every finite‑dimensional irreducible representation of G as the space of global sections of a suitable line bundle (Borel–Weil) and extends the picture to higher cohomology (Bott). The proof uses the Leray spectral sequence for the projection G/B → G/P together with the Kodaira vanishing theorem on the fibres, or alternatively the algebraic approach via the BGG resolution. The theorem also holds for algebraic groups over algebraically closed fields of characteristic zero, and in positive characteristic it fails in general, leading to the theory of Frobenius twists and the linkage principle.

Geometric Consequences

The flag variety G/B is a smooth projective variety whose Picard group is the weight lattice. The ample line bundles correspond to dominant regular weights; their global sections give the projective embedding of G/B via the very ample line bundle L(ρ). The cohomology vanishing for dominant weights (Kempf vanishing) is a special case of the theorem. Moreover, the Euler characteristic of L(λ) equals the Weyl character formula evaluated at the formal exponential of λ, providing a geometric proof of the character formula.

Kazhdan–Lusztig Polynomials and Intersection Cohomology of Schubert Varieties

For each element w of the Weyl group W, the Schubert variety X_w is the closure of the Bruhat cell BwB/B in the flag variety G/B. These varieties are typically singular, and their ordinary cohomology does not satisfy Poincaré duality. The intersection cohomology groups IHⁱ(X_w) restore duality and carry a natural action of the Hecke algebra. Kazhdan and Lusztig introduced polynomials Py,w(q) (with y ≤ w in Bruhat order) which encode the dimensions of the stalks of the intersection cohomology complexes. The coefficients of Py,w are non‑negative integers; Pw,w=1 and deg Py,w ≤ (ℓ(w)−ℓ(y)−1)/2. The Kazhdan–Lusztig conjecture (proved by Beilinson–Bernstein and Brylinski–Kashiwara) states that the multiplicity of the simple highest‑weight module L(y·λ) in the Verma module M(w·λ) equals the coefficient of q(ℓ(w)−ℓ(y))/2 in Py,w(q). This gives a precise link between representation theory of the Lie algebra 𝔤 and the geometry of Schubert varieties. The polynomials can be computed recursively using the Hecke algebra relations T_s T_w = Tsw + (q−1)T_w for simple reflections s with ℓ(sw) > ℓ(w). The inverse Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials Qy,w appear in the expansion of the canonical basis of the Hecke algebra. In type A, the polynomials have a combinatorial interpretation via the Robinson–Schensted correspondence and the theory of standard Young tableaux.

Worked Example: Computing a Kazhdan–Lusztig Polynomial in Type A₂
Let W = S₃ with simple reflections s₁, s₂. Compute Pe, w₀(q) where w₀ = s₁s₂s₁ is the longest element.
The Bruhat order in S₃ has elements e, s₁, s₂, s₁s₂, s₂s₁, w₀. The recursive definition: for y < w, Py,w = 0 if y ≰ w. For w = w₀, we use the formula Py,w = Psy, sw when sy < y and sw < w. Taking s = s₁, we have s₁w₀ = s₂s₁ and s₁e = s₁. Since s₁ > e, we use the alternative recursion: Pe,w₀ = Ps₁, s₂s₁. Repeating with s = s₂ gives Ps₁, s₂s₁ = Ps₂s₁, s₂s₁ = 1. Hence Pe,w₀(q) = 1. This matches the known fact that all Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials in rank two are 1.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the cohomology of a line bundle on a flag variety, first check whether the weight is dominant after the dot shift. If it is singular, answer zero immediately. If regular, find the unique Weyl group element making it dominant; the cohomology lives in degree equal to the length of that element. For Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials, remember that in simply‑laced types of rank ≤ 3 all polynomials are 1; higher ranks require the recursive Hecke algebra computation.

The Harish‑Chandra Isomorphism and the Center of the Universal Enveloping Algebra

The center Z(𝔤) of the universal enveloping algebra U(𝔤) of a complex semisimple Lie algebra 𝔤 is isomorphic, via the Harish‑Chandra homomorphism, to the algebra of Weyl‑group invariant polynomials on a Cartan subalgebra 𝔥. Explicitly, the map γ: Z(𝔤) → S(𝔥)^W is defined by projecting an element of U(𝔤) onto its component in the Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt decomposition U(𝔤) = U(𝔥) ⊕ (𝔫⁻U(𝔤) + U(𝔤)𝔫⁺) and then shifting by the half‑sum of positive roots ρ: for z ∈ Z(𝔤), write z = p(𝔥) + … and set γ(z)(λ) = p(λ+ρ). The image is exactly the W-invariant subalgebra S(𝔥)^W. This isomorphism identifies the infinitesimal character of a highest‑weight module V(λ) with the evaluation of invariant polynomials at λ+ρ. Consequently, two highest‑weight modules have the same infinitesimal character iff their highest weights lie in the same dot‑orbit of the Weyl group. The Harish‑Chandra isomorphism is the algebraic counterpart of the fact that the algebra of invariant differential operators on the symmetric space G/K is polynomial. It also underlies the construction of the Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand category 𝒪 and the linkage principle: blocks of category 𝒪 correspond to orbits of the dot action on the weight lattice.

Applications to Invariant Theory

The isomorphism shows that Z(𝔤) is a polynomial algebra in rank(𝔤) generators, the fundamental invariants. For 𝔰𝔩_n these are the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial of a generic matrix, i.e. the traces of powers. The Capelli identities give explicit central elements in U(𝔰𝔩_n) corresponding to these invariants. In the quantum group setting, the Harish‑Chandra map deforms to an isomorphism between the center of the quantum enveloping algebra U_q(𝔤) and the algebra of quantum invariant polynomials, with the shift by ρ replaced by the quantum Weyl vector.

Van Est Isomorphism and Continuous Group Cohomology

For a connected Lie group G with Lie algebra 𝔤, the Van Est theorem relates the continuous group cohomology H^•_c(G, V) (with coefficients in a smooth G-module V) to the Lie algebra cohomology H^•(𝔤, V). If G is simply connected, the natural map induced by differentiation is an isomorphism in all degrees. For a general connected G, the map is an isomorphism up to degree k where k is the connectivity of the maximal compact subgroup K (i.e. π_i(K)=0 for i ≤ k). In particular, for a semisimple group with finite center, the maximal compact subgroup is a compact Lie group of the same rank, so the map is an isomorphism in degrees ≤ 2. The proof uses the spectral sequence associated to the fibration G → G/K and the fact that the homogeneous space G/K is contractible. The Van Est isomorphism is a key tool in the computation of the cohomology of arithmetic groups via the Matsushima formula, which expresses the cohomology of a locally symmetric space ΓG/K in terms of the (𝔤, K)-cohomology of automorphic representations. It also underlies the comparison between group cohomology and the cohomology of the classifying space BG.

Relation to Characteristic Classes

Via the Chern–Weil homomorphism, invariant polynomials on 𝔤 give characteristic classes of principal G-bundles. The Van Est isomorphism identifies these classes with elements of H^•(BG, ℝ). For a compact group, the cohomology ring H^•(BG, ℝ) is isomorphic to the algebra of invariant polynomials S(𝔤^*)^G, which by the Harish‑Chandra isomorphism is the same as S(𝔥^*)^W. Thus the characteristic classes of a compact Lie group are polynomial functions on the Cartan subalgebra invariant under the Weyl group.

The Duflo Isomorphism and Primitive Ideals in Enveloping Algebras

The Duflo isomorphism extends the Harish‑Chandra isomorphism to a canonical algebra isomorphism between the center Z(𝔤) and the invariant subalgebra S(𝔤)^𝔤 of the symmetric algebra, not just the Cartan part. It is constructed by composing the symmetrisation map sym: S(𝔤) → U(𝔤) with the action of the square root of the Todd class j1/2(x) = det1/2((sinh(ad x/2))/(ad x/2)). The resulting map D: S(𝔤)^𝔤 → Z(𝔤) is an algebra isomorphism, whereas the symmetrisation map alone is only a vector space isomorphism and fails to respect multiplication. The Duflo map is natural with respect to Lie algebra homomorphisms and plays a central role in the orbit method: for a nilpotent Lie algebra, the Kirillov character formula identifies the primitive ideals of U(𝔤) with the annihilators of irreducible representations, which correspond to coadjoint orbits. Duflo's theorem shows that the associated graded of a primitive ideal (with respect to the natural filtration) is the defining ideal of the closure of the corresponding coadjoint orbit in 𝔤^*. This gives a geometric classification of primitive ideals for nilpotent and, more generally, for solvable Lie algebras. For semisimple 𝔤, the Joseph ideal (the unique completely prime primitive ideal of minimal Gelfand–Kirillov dimension) is the annihilator of the minimal representation; its associated variety is the minimal nilpotent orbit.

Quantum Duflo Isomorphism

In the quantum group U_q(𝔤), an analogue of the Duflo isomorphism exists, relating the quantum center to the quantum invariant subalgebra of the quantum symmetric algebra. The quantum Todd class involves the quantum determinant and the ribbon element. This quantum Duflo map is essential for the construction of the Drinfeld double and for the categorification of quantum groups via the geometry of the affine Grassmannian.

  • Assuming the Borel–Weil–Bott theorem holds in positive characteristic without Frobenius twists; it fails in general and requires the linkage principle.
  • Confusing the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomial Py,w with its inverse Qy,w; they satisfy different recursions and have distinct representation‑theoretic meanings.
  • Thinking the Harish‑Chandra isomorphism identifies Z(𝔤) with S(𝔥) without the Weyl‑group invariance and the ρ-shift; the shift is essential for matching infinitesimal characters.
  • Applying the Van Est isomorphism to a non‑connected group without passing to the identity component; the theorem requires connectedness (or a correction by group cohomology of the component group).
  • Believing the symmetrisation map S(𝔤) → U(𝔤) is an algebra isomorphism; it is only a coalgebra isomorphism, and the Duflo correction is needed for the center.
  • Overlooking that the Joseph ideal exists only for simple Lie algebras of type not A₁; for 𝔰𝔩₂ the minimal representation is the adjoint representation and the primitive ideal structure differs.
  • Mixing the dot action w·λ = w(λ+ρ)−ρ with the ordinary Weyl action; the dot action governs linkage in category 𝒪 and the Borel–Weil–Bott theorem.

The Orbit Method and Kirillov Character Formula for Nilpotent Lie Groups

The orbit method, introduced by Kirillov in the early 1960s, provides a geometric parametrisation of the unitary dual of a simply connected nilpotent Lie group G. The starting point is the coadjoint action of G on the dual space 𝔤* of its Lie algebra.
